The line stating that the eight worldly concerns "do not arise" for beings who delight in "that" is translated as written; the following verses describe how these eight harm worldly beings, so the referent of "that" (the conventional teaching or the Buddha's teaching) is somewhat unclear. "The four hundred million" renders forty koṭis. "Equal to the Hosts of Stars" renders སྐར་ཚོགས་མཉམ་པ, taken here as the name of the eon; it could instead be descriptive (eons as numerous as the stars). "Pure Intellect" translates བློ་གྲོས་དག, the name under which the future buddhas will be known; no Sanskrit form is asserted. The line about twenty koṭis "twenty upon twenty" (ཉི་ཤུ་ཉི་ཤུས) is somewhat obscure and may mean twenty koṭis in successive groups of twenty. The text reads ཞན ("weak, inferior"), which does not fit; it has been read as ཞེན ("cling"), giving "ordinary beings cling to them in another way." In this section ལུང་བསྟན་པ refers to the Buddha's prophecy of beings to unsurpassed awakening, continuing the preceding discussion, and is rendered "prophecy." In the verse beginning "And whoever practices the aggregates," the Tibetan reads "aggregates alike to elaboration," which sits oddly beside the following "free of conceptual elaboration"; a reading such as "aggregates free of elaboration" may be intended, but the text has been rendered as written. The humble verb translated "understand" (and once "seek") can mean either; it is chosen by context. The buddha-name rendered "Kings Gentle in the Self-Arisen Dharma" is uncertain: the Tibetan reads "rnam des," which may be a slip for "rnam nges," "certain," which would give "Kings Certain in the Self-Arisen Dharma." The line "In their own ground, its approaches not left empty" renders the obscure ཉེ་ལག་སྟོང་མ་ཡིན་པར; ཉེ་ལག may mean the approach or preliminary stage (sāmantaka), and the sense is uncertain. In the austerity verse, ད་ལྟར་མིན is read as "not like this," taking ད་ལྟར as a slip for དེ་ལྟར. The number ཁྲག་ཁྲིག is a very large numeral whose exact value varies between sources; "trillion" is approximate. "Limit of reality" renders ཡང་དག་པའི་མཐའ (bhūtakoṭi). The number ཁྲག་ཁྲིག is a very large numeral of uncertain exact value; "eighty trillion" follows a common convention. The word ཐ་བ in "undefiled and stainless" is obscure; it is taken here as a term for impurity or defect. Following the established name list, the class of gods is rendered "Vaśavartin class" (the heaven otherwise called Paranirmitavaśavartin), and their king དབང་སྒྱུར is also rendered Vaśavartin. The verse line rendered "You of meaningful renown" is terse in the Tibetan and its exact sense is uncertain. In the list at the end the Tibetan repeats the word for "coming" in the pair "neither gone nor come"; it is rendered as it stands. In Brahmā's first verse the text reads ཆང་བ་སྟོང ("empty beer vessel"), which makes little sense in a list of similes; it is translated as the evident reading ཁང་བ་སྟོང "empty house," a standard simile for emptiness. In the earlier verse, the line rendered "they will become guides skilled in method, honored with supreme offerings" could also be read as "they will honor the Guide skilled in method with supreme offerings." The name of the future buddhas, ཡེ་ཤེས་སྟོབས་ལྡན, is translated by meaning as "Possessor of the Power of Wisdom"; a Sanskrit form such as Jñānabala cannot be confirmed. The "one kind of suffering" is the suffering of formations, the all-pervasive suffering of conditioned existence, as distinct from the suffering of change and the suffering of suffering, which the following lines say will be absent. The word འགྲེལ in "relieving the suffering of the world" is read in the sense of loosening or relieving. The term ཤོ་བེ in the list of attributes of a is rare; it is rendered "deceptive" from context (paired with "not false, not true"), but the exact sense is uncertain. The "voice endowed with eight qualities" renders ཡན་ལག་བརྒྱད, the traditional eight qualities of a buddha's or Brahmā's voice. The verse line rendered "phenomena have no nature of reality" translates the Tibetan literally (phenomena are "without dharmatā"); the sense is presumably that not even the nature of reality exists as a real entity. In the closing stanza the line about the Sugata being "perfectly honored with the Dharma" leaves the agent unstated in the Tibetan; it may be read as the gods' offering of Dharma. "Eight qualities" refers to the eight-limbed voice of a buddha. The line "Rain down the nectar..." could also be read as "those who thirst will receive it." The final verses of praise after the gods' homage are not closed by a separate quotation marker in the Tibetan, so they are taken here as a continuation of the gods' words. The rendering "bringing beyond sorrow" reads the unusual phrase mya ngan bzlas as referring to nirvāṇa. "Mog gcod" in the execution clause is read as srog gcod, "to put to death," which fits the context and matches the later phrase "take my life in every way." Here dbang lnga means the five masteries or powers of a bodhisattva, not ritual empowerment. Several items in the list of tortures, such as "rolled up like a dried ball" and "heated like a warming pot," are obscure and are rendered literally. In the list "no sentient being, no life, no human, no nourisher, no person," ཤེད་བུ and གསོ་བ are read as the standard renderings of manuja and poṣa in the usual list of synonyms for a self. The verses then run through the four immeasurables, the four applications of mindfulness, the bases of miraculous power, the five faculties, the five powers, the seven limbs of awakening, and the eightfold path ("twice four"), each affirmed conventionally and denied ultimately. The number ཁྲག་ཁྲིག, a very large numeral, is rendered "billions" throughout. The word ཁུམ in "dispel my doubt" is unusual and is read from context as removing or subduing doubt. "Eight hundred million" renders "eighty koṭis." The eleven kinds of eloquence (pratibhāna) are rendered by their plain sense; "apposite" for ཉེ་བ (literally "near") is an interpretive choice. "Clear sensitivity" renders དང་བ (Skt. prasāda), the subtle sense-matter of the eye formed from the elements. "Empty fist" renders ཆང་པ་སྟོང་པ (Skt. riktamuṣṭi), the standard image of something that seems to hold something but holds nothing. The phrase བས་ཀྱི in the final sentence is taken as an abbreviated or slipped form of བགྱིས་ཀྱི, "is designated, but." The term rendered "without association" (འདྲིས་པ་མ་མཆིས་པ) is somewhat uncertain; it may also mean "without mixture" or "without familiarity." In the list of negations, the Tibetan reads "consciousness" where every parallel pair has a negated form ("not cognized, cannot be cognized"). It is translated as the evident negated reading, which is likely a scribal slip. The clause "the result of the path is not cessation" is translated as written. It apparently means that, with no afflictions to abandon, there is no cessation to serve as the path's result. The phrase rendered "it is so in mere name" reads མིང་ཙམ་དུ་བས་ཀྱི, where བས is evidently a slip for a verb such as བགྱིས or ལགས; the sense is unaffected. The sentence "there is no alteration in the dharmadhātu, and it is likewise impossible..." renders a terse construction (literally "there is no change in the dharmadhātu, and also there is"). It is taken here as linking the unchanging dharmadhātu to the certainty of those beings' awakening. The verses on the movements of the smile's light (circling swiftly or slowly, "pressing down," "a parasol with endless eyes") are terse and their referents somewhat obscure; they are rendered closely to the Tibetan. ཁུམ is taken as an imperative "quell, subdue" (doubts). The buddha name "Glory of the Disk of Sunlight" and the eon name "Golden Banner" are translated literally, since a Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. Most of the gods' names in the praise section are likewise given by meaning: Devoted to Generosity, Endowed with Splendor, Intelligence of the Elements, Mind Ornament, Swift Action, and the others. The epithet rendered "you who hold all embodied beings" (gdos 'dzin) is uncertain. The verse of the god Intelligence of the Elements lists the four means of attracting disciples: giving, pleasant speech, beneficial conduct, and consistency. The names of the many gods (devaputras) in this sequence are Tibetan translations whose Sanskrit originals cannot be identified with confidence, so their meanings have been rendered in English. "Imperturbable" renders the same Tibetan word that elsewhere stands for Akṣobhya; here it names a god, not the buddha. The line rendered "Reversing their way" is terse; it may mean that the Buddha teaches in a manner contrary to ordinary worldly views. The names of the gods are descriptive and have been translated into English in keeping with the earlier pieces. Two names occur twice in this piece, "Delighting in Insight" and "Arisen from Insight". Each repeat is rendered the same way as its first occurrence. In the verse of Holding to Conscientiousness, "never grew weary" takes skyor as skyo ("to weary"). In the verse of Gone Forth through Insight, the last line is obscure; "no recollection of that attainment arises" is a tentative reading. The final verse continues into the next piece. The speakers are young gods whose names are formed from the themes of insight, emptiness and signlessness; no Sanskrit forms can be reconstructed with confidence, so their names are translated by meaning. Two speakers have nearly identical names (one with བསྟེན, one with སྟེན); they are distinguished as "Relying on Emptiness" and "Depending on Emptiness." The names of the gods' sons are descriptive Tibetan names that cannot be confidently identified with Sanskrit forms; they are translated by meaning. In the verse of Free of Attachment to the Body, "Buddha who expounds" renders rnam 'grel as written; it may be a slip for rnam grol, "fully liberated." The names of the many gods (devaputras) in this passage are descriptive Tibetan names whose Sanskrit originals cannot be confidently reconstructed, so they are translated by meaning. "Lord of Men" (mi dbang) is taken as an epithet of the Buddha. The last verse's "through a fearlessness in which nothing is attained" renders a terse line (thob med pas) that could also be read as "because they have attained fearlessness without attainment." The names of the gods who speak these verses are descriptive Tibetan renderings that cannot be confidently traced to established Sanskrit forms, so they are translated by meaning. The Tibetan word for "abode" also means "ground, possibility." In the verse on the possible and the impossible, which alludes to the first of the ten powers, it is rendered accordingly, and in the verses on karma it is rendered "basis." The final verse continues in the next piece. The names of the gods' sons are translated literally, as their Sanskrit originals cannot be identified with confidence. These verses track the ten powers of a tathāgata, especially the knowledge of karma's undertaking and ripening, the knowledge of the various elements, and the knowledge of the paths that lead everywhere. "Undertaking" renders ལེན་པ in its sense of the undertaking of actions (samādāna) in that formula. The "three groups" are the three classes of beings: those fixed in wrongness, those fixed in rightness, and the undetermined. In the verse spoken by Fearless on the Path, the first line names only the group fixed in wrongness. The names of the many gods' sons are translated by meaning, as their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence. "Renounced through This Condition" alludes to idampratyayatā, the specific conditionality of dependent origination. In the verse of Delighting in the Faculties, the text reads ཉོན་མོངས་སྙོན་མོངས་; the second word is taken as a slip, and the line is read as affliction paired with purification. "Certain of the right" and "certain of the wrong" render the two fixed groups of beings, those destined for liberation and those fixed in wrongdoing. The names of the many gods' sons in this sequence are descriptive and cannot be identified with known Sanskrit forms; they are translated by meaning. "The six streams of existence" renders rgyud drug, here evidently the six destinies of rebirth, not tantra. The verse "In the past you gave various gifts" is syntactically compressed and its construal is somewhat uncertain. The names of the gods' sons (devaputras) are descriptive Tibetan names that cannot be confidently traced to established Sanskrit forms, so they are translated by meaning. The first line completes a verse begun in the previous piece. The names of the many gods in this section are Tibetan renderings of Sanskrit names that cannot be reconstructed with confidence, so they are translated by meaning. The name read here as "Crossed Beyond the Swamp" is written with a syllable that makes no sense; it has been taken as the word for "swamp," which the same god's verse uses twice. In the verse of the god Skilled in Conceptualization, the word rendered "kinsfolk" is obscure in this list, and the rendering is literal. In the verse of the god Free of Craving, "having put an end to further existence" renders an unclear verb taken in that sense. The opening verse continues the speech of the god's son introduced at the end of the previous piece. The names of the gods' sons are translated by meaning because their Sanskrit originals cannot be identified with confidence. The verb written བས, as in "removed" or "eliminated", appears to mean "removed, exhausted." In the eighth verse, མ་ལུས་པས is read as མ་ལུས་བས, "removed without remainder," which fits the parallel lines. The names of the many gods (devaputras) in this passage cannot be confidently matched to Sanskrit forms, so they are translated by meaning, following the convention already used for Pure Mind. In the verse of the god Knower of Virtue and Nonvirtue, the word rendered "takes up" literally means "examined" or "imputed"; the sense appears to be adopting or engaging with what is afflicted. The devaputra names are translated by meaning, as no Sanskrit forms can be identified with confidence. In the name rendered "Steadfast in Reality," the element རྟུལ is uncertain (perhaps for བརྟུལ or a related word), and the name in "Conjuring in Former Times" (འཕྲུལ་པ) is likewise uncertain. The opening verse belongs to the devaputra Dismantler of Perceptions, whose introduction ends the previous piece. The names of the many young gods are translated by meaning, since their Sanskrit originals cannot be identified with confidence. "Savior of the World" renders an epithet of the Buddha here, not the goddess Tārā. The term rendered "garland-bearing elephant riders" is uncertain; it seems to name one class of spirits, perhaps the Mālādhara attendants of the Four Great Kings. The vessel of sesame oil alludes to the image of carrying a full vessel of oil without spilling a drop, a picture of unbroken mindfulness. The phrase rendered "You have been ruined by those who are themselves ruined" renders a terse Tibetan expression, literally "consumed by the consumed." It most naturally means that the ascetics have been led to ruin by teachers who were themselves ruined. "Ford-maker" renders the term that elsewhere in this text designates non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). Here it is used in its root sense, tīrthakara, "one who makes a ford," as part of the Buddha's play on fords and ferrying across, so the literal sense has been kept. "The way out" renders a term that also means "emergence" or "deliverance" (probably niḥsaraṇa). The "embryo" is the earliest embryonic stage (kalala). Some vocal qualities in the list of signs ("must be puzzled out," "booming") are uncertain renderings, as is "overpowered by dogs," where the original may describe being seized or attacked by them. "Wanderers" renders ཀུན་ཏུ་རྒྱུ (parivrājaka), wandering ascetics who are being addressed. The world of Yama here designates the realm of pretas, or hungry ghosts. The asura trait བད་ག་གཟེངས་སྟོད་པ is read as བདག་གཟེངས་བསྟོད་པ, "self-exalting, boastful." Some items in the lists, such as "desires distinction" for མངོན་རྟགས་འདོད་པ and "fond of going about" for འགྲོ་བའི་ངང་ཚུལ་ཅན, are tentative renderings of terse expressions. "Holding to be supreme" renders the Tibetan for clinging to one's own practice as the highest (Skt. parāmarśa, as in śīlavrataparāmarśa). "The world of Yama" is the realm of departed spirits (pretas), to which miserliness leads. "Go forth to omniscience" renders ངེས་པར་འབྱུང་བ, which can also be read as "definitively attain" or "emerge into" omniscience. The phrase "as he goes about there and follows it through with familiarization" interprets a terse Tibetan construction. "Sovereign over the whole collection of dharmas" reads the evident མངའ་དབང ("sovereignty") for the written མདའ་དབང, which makes no sense here. In the verse on the fearlessnesses, སྔོ་བའི་དགྲ is unclear; it is rendered as "the foes who challenged him," taking it as a slip for རྒོལ་བའི་དགྲ ("opposing foes"). Cakradhara ("wheel-holder") most likely refers to Viṣṇu, and Īśvara to the creator god (Śiva). "Victorious over Foes" and "Destroyer of Pride" translate names of buddhas whose Sanskrit forms I could not identify with confidence. The line "grievous kinds of views" renders a phrase whose wording is unusual; the sense is that these beings had held harmful wrong views. In the closing verse, the abbreviated epithet is read as "the Victor who destroyed pride," describing the Buddha Śākyamuni as speaker. In the list of watery substances the text reads "dust" (rdul), which makes no sense there; it is evidently a slip for "sweat" (rngul), and is translated so. Several of the body parts in the earth list (stomach, pleura, mesentery, brain membrane) are standard but approximate identifications. Among the mountains, "Vajra Holders" and "Bare Peaks" translate names I could not confidently identify with a Sanskrit form. In the simile of the raindrops, the Tibetan reads "on the great" without a noun; this is taken as "on the great earth," assuming a dropped syllable for "earth." "Palm tree" renders the tāla tree used as a measure of height. ཁོང་སྟོད in the description of the internal space element is read as ཁོང་སྟོང "hollow," the reading found in the parallel phrase for the external space element; "upper torso" makes little sense in a list of cavities. "Dominant condition" and "object" render bdag po and dmigs pa (adhipati and ālambana). "Clear matter" renders dang ba (prasāda), the subtle translucent matter that constitutes the sense organs. "Impinge" renders thogs pa (pratigha), and "alighting" renders 'bab pa. Here mtshan ma is "sign" (nimitta), not the major marks of a great being. The addressee "Great King" is King Śuddhodana, the Buddha's father, as throughout this sūtra. In the passage on rebirth, the term usually meaning "empowerment" is rendered here as "governing condition" (adhipati), its Abhidharma sense of one of the conditions, since the text speaks of "two conditions." The phrase rendered "not over there" reads oddly in the Tibetan; it is taken as a variant of the word for "beyond, over there." In the first of the two parallel passages, the Tibetan reads "the final consciousness is empty of all consciousness," whereas the second passage has the expected "empty of the final consciousness." This may be a scribal variant, but it is translated as written.

The term rendered "force" (literally "power") in "the force of the final consciousness" is used here in its ordinary sense, not in the ritual sense of empowerment. In this passage the glossary words with several senses are used in their non-ritual senses. "Dominance" renders the term the glossary gives as "empowerment"; here it means the last consciousness acting as the dominant condition (adhipatipratyaya) for rebirth. "Mind-stream" renders the term the glossary gives as "tantra"; here it means the mental continuum (saṃtāna). The word the glossary gives as "mantra" appears here only inside the compound for "lamenting." In the first of the two parallel passages, the Tibetan reads "Karma, ripening, and the first consciousness are empty of the intrinsic nature of a first consciousness," compressing what the second passage spells out item by item; this has been translated as it stands. The text as transmitted reads ལས་རྣམས་ཆུད་ཟ་ཞིང, literally "karmas are wasted," which contradicts the point being made, that karma still bears fruit. The Sanskrit of this passage, as quoted in the Śikṣāsamuccaya, has "karma is not lost" (karmaṇām avipraṇāśaḥ). The translation follows that sense, taking the Tibetan as a slip for ཆུད་མི་ཟ. In the phrase "the final consciousness as the dominant condition," the Tibetan དབང is not "empowerment." Here it means dominance or governing power, the dominant condition (adhipatipratyaya) of Abhidharma, paired with karma as the object condition. The doubled ཉལ་ཉལ་བ is read as a simple repetition, "one who has slept and woken." The three bitter seeds are plant names: the bitter gourd, kośātakī (a bitter luffa-like gourd), and picumarda, the neem tree. In the second example "neem" renders a spelling with an initial p instead of b; these are the same word. "Of corresponding share" and "of corresponding kind" render the Tibetan for Sanskrit sabhāga. The king addressed throughout is the Buddha's father, King Śuddhodana, who is not named in this piece. Here དབང is rendered "dominant influence" (the dominant condition, adhipati) rather than the glossary's ritual sense "empowerment," which does not fit this context. The phrase "the most beautiful woman in the land" renders ཡུལ་གྱི་བཟང་མོ (Skt. janapadakalyāṇī). "Fascicle" renders the Tibetan division marker bampo, which falls mid-discourse here. "Wishlessness" renders the third door of liberation (apraṇihita). In the first occurrence of the formula, the Tibetan lists the realms of rebirth without stating the verb "arises"; the parallel later passage supplies it, and the translation adds it in both places. The phrase "does not abide supported in the east, and so on" abbreviates the standard list of the directions. Here དབང་ཕྱུག is not the name Īśvara but the common noun "sovereignty, lordship" (aiśvarya), since the Buddha is warning the king against attachment to royal power; it is translated accordingly. The king's name གྲགས་པ་མཐའ་ཡས is rendered Anantayaśas ("Boundless Fame"); Anantakīrti is an equally possible Sanskrit form. The name of the capital, rendered literally as "Adorned with Jewels," likely reflects a Sanskrit form such as Ratnālaṃkṛtā, but this could not be confirmed, so the meaning is translated. Among the seven precious substances, "agate" and "emerald" render the traditional equivalents of Tibetan spug (musāragalva) and rdo'i snying po (aśmagarbha), whose exact mineral identities are debated. The king's name གྲགས་པ་མཐའ་ཡས is rendered Anantayaśas ("Boundless Fame"), the standard Sanskrit equivalent; this is the figure known elsewhere in the Māndhātṛ type of story. Several flower names (མ་ཡུ་ཀ, ད་ནུ་སྐ་རི) are transliterated as spelled since they cannot be confidently identified. ཁོར་ཡུག here means "all around" rather than the Cakravāla mountain range. The names Nandaka (དགའ་བྱེད) and Bhūdeva (སའི་ལྷ) are probable reconstructions; the father's name ས་འབྱུང, spelled once as རྒྱལ་པོས་འབྱུང (read as རྒྱལ་པོ་ས་འབྱུང), could not be identified with confidence and is translated as "Earth-Arising." The city name "Jewel-Adorned" and the park name "Jewel Casket" are translated from the Tibetan. The king's name སའི་ལྷ is rendered Bhūdeva; the Sanskrit is a reconstruction ("God of the Earth") and may correspond to Pṛthivīdeva in other sources. The father's name ས་འབྱུང་ in the preceding context is likewise uncertain. The term སྤུག among the seven jewels is rendered by its Mahāvyutpatti equivalent musāragalva, a stone variously identified as agate or cat's eye. Following the context, the three remaining "illnesses" are taken as desire, hunger (literally "not eating food"), and old age. The king's name སའི་ལྷ has been rendered Bhūdeva, a literal Sanskrit equivalent of "Earth God"; the Sanskrit original of this name is not securely attested and should be harmonized with the form used in earlier pieces. The city name བདེ་བའི་ཁྱིམ could not be identified with a known Sanskrit name and is translated by meaning as "Happy Abode." The clause about splendor is somewhat terse; it is taken to mean that, coming from divine splendor, the king could not bear the human condition and therefore sank into the earth. The city name rendered literally "Unassailable" is taken as Ayodhyā, of which the Tibetan is a standard rendering. The three palace names (Moonrise, Essence of Beryl, Lofty Sunbeams) are translated from their meanings, as their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. The city name "Unassailable" renders མི་ཚུགས་པ literally; it may stand for an Indian name (possibly Ayodhyā, "the unconquerable"), but this cannot be confirmed. In "held sway over half the kingdom" the text reads བྱེད, which is taken as a slip for ཕྱེད "half," matching the preceding sentence. In the verses, གླིང་བཞི་པ་ལ་རབ་བསྟན is read as རབ་བསྟེན "enjoyed," as in the parallel line earlier. མུ་ཁྱུད ("rim, felly") is taken as King Nemi, the king whom Mātali brings to the Heaven of the Thirty-Three in the parallel story. ང་ལས་ནུ (and ང་ནུ in the verse) is the standard Tibetan rendering of Māndhātā. The large number བྱེ་བ་ཁྲག་ཁྲིག་བརྒྱ་སྟོང་ཕྲག་བརྒྱད་ཅུ is rendered with the conventional formula "eighty hundred thousand koṭis of niyutas." The Tibetan name Mukhyü renders Sanskrit Nemi, literally "wheel rim," the king known in Pāli as Nimi, whom Mātali likewise carries to heaven by way of the hells. "Error" renders the Buddhist technical term for inversion or distorted cognition (viparyāsa). "The meditative absorption of the definitive teaching of reality" echoes the sūtra's own title phrase. The text announces "seventy gateways of aspects," but the list as it stands does not tally exactly to seventy; it is translated as written, including the repeated "signless" and "without going." The lines "for the sake of awakening, as buddhas, / guiding beings toward the buddhas' intent" render a terse Tibetan couplet whose syntax is ambiguous; the general sense is that these future buddhas tame beings in accordance with the buddhas' intention.
